A junior dev or agent could execute these without understanding the broader system. **Phase 2A** (toast migration) is also solid — exact file list, clear find-and-replace pattern. **Phase 5** (wontfixes) is fully mechanical — just copy-paste commands. ## What would trip up an agent **Phase 2B–2D** starts getting fuzzy: - "Find optimistic update `onError` callbacks" — which ones? Needs judgment. - **3E** (preset controller decomposition) — "consolidate 3 overlapping useEffects into 1" is a real refactoring task requiring understanding of timing/ordering. Not mechanical at all. - **3F** is a grab-bag with vague instructions like "extract shared popover form content." **Phase 4** is architect-level work dressed up as a task list: - "Group 22 fields into `{ formState, promptConfig, effects }`" — deciding which fields go where requires understanding the domain. - The `debugConfig` Proxy pattern in 4B is clever but fragile if an agent doesn't understand why. ## Verdict **Phases 1–2A: safe to hand off.** Clear, mechanical, well-specified. **Phases 2B–2E: needs a capable agent** that can read surrounding code and make small judgment calls, but the instructions give enough direction. **Phases 3–4: needs a senior dev or very thorough agent** with the ability to read the codebase, understand hook composition, and make interface design decisions. The plan gives good *direction* but not enough *specification* for truly mechanical execution. If you want to make 3–4 more code-monkey proof, the main gap is: enumerate the exact lines/blocks to change (like Phase 1 does), and provide complete code snippets for the extracted pieces rather than partial examples.

But looking at the signature: ```ts export async function createGenerationFromTask( supabase: SupabaseClient, taskId: string, taskData: unknown, publicUrl: string, thumbnailUrl: string | null | undefined, logger?: CompletionLogger, authContext: CompletionAuthContext, ): Promise<GenerationCreationOutcome> ``` The `logger` parameter is optional (`logger?`), followed by a required `authContext`. This means if a caller passes 6 args (omitting logger), `authContext` would be the 7th positional arg and would be `undefined`. This is a problematic positional signature: an optional parameter before a required one. TypeScript should catch this at call sites, but the pattern is a contract smell. This is semantically odd: success results don't need a `recoverable` field. It's there presumably to satisfy discriminated union checks, but it creates a confusing API - callers seeing `recoverable: false` on a success result might reasonably ask "what wasn't recoverable?" The contract mixes failure-semantics fields into the success type. Now let me check the `calculateTaskCost` rounding inconsistency: In `costHelpers.ts`: - `calculateVideoEnhanceCost` rounds to 6 decimal places (`Math.round(totalCost * 1000000) / 1000000`) - `calculateTaskCost` rounds to 3 decimal places (`Math.round(totalCost * 1000) / 1000`) This is a contract coherence issue: `calculateTaskCost` delegates to `calculateVideoEnhanceCost` for `video_enhance` tasks, which rounds to 6 decimals, but all other billing types round to 3 decimals. Now let me summarize my findings and write the output. The key things I found: **Positives (not to report, but context for scoring):** - Supabase client uses proper lazy initialization with Result pattern - Env reads via `import.meta.env` are build-time replaced by Vite (not true runtime coupling) - Debug config uses lazy initialization - Toast runtime, viewport lock runtime, error presenter all use explicit init patterns - Bootstrap has a clear initialization order with idempotency guards **Defects found:** 1. `autoplayMonitor.ts` - calls `startAutoplayMonitoring()` at module scope (import-time side effect with `setInterval`) 2. `debugPolling.ts` - attaches to `window` at module scope (import-time side effect) 3. `mobileProjectDebug.ts` - attaches to `window` at module scope (import-time side effect) 4. `simpleCacheValidator.ts` - attaches to `window` at module scope (import-time side effect) 5. `logger.ts` - captures `console.log/warn/error` at module scope; ordering matters relative to any other console-overwriting code 6. `__IS_DEV_ENV__` in `env.ts` - computed constant at import time that reads `window.location.hostname`; consumers use the stale snapshot instead of a function call 7. `settingsWriteQueue.ts` - `enqueueSettingsWrite` throws if `setSettingsWriteFunction` wasn't called first - implicit boot ordering dependency 8. `DataFreshnessManager`, `RealtimeEventProcessor`, `queryClient` - module-scope singletons instantiated at import time The dynamically-imported debug tools (items 1-4) are all guarded by `import.meta.env.DEV` and loaded lazily from bootstrap, which mitigates but doesn't eliminate the coupling. The more significant findings are the `originalConsole` capture ordering, the `__IS_DEV_ENV__` snapshot constant, and the `settingsWriteQueue` boot-order dependency.

However, there are meaningful inconsistencies: 1. **Bypasses of the error system** - `useSegmentMutations.ts` (11 raw console.error calls, zero normalized errors), `useTrainingDataUpload.ts` (4 console.error+throw patterns), and `useVariants.ts` all bypass the centralized error pipeline. 2. **Mixed strategies within single files** - `useShotImageMutations.ts` uses `toast.error` for some mutation errors and `normalizeAndPresentError` for others in the same file. 3. **Dual error paradigm** - `OperationResult` pattern (~25 files) coexists with throw+catch (~250+ files) without clear boundaries for when to use which. 4. **Silent failures** - `useReplaceInShot` and `useShareGeneration` swallow DB errors with no user feedback and limited/no logging. 5. **Typed errors underused** - The `AuthError`/`NetworkError`/`ServerError` hierarchy exists but only ~12 throw sites use it vs ~214 using bare `new Error`, relying on message-string-matching in `categorizeError()` to reconstruct types.
